Rebuilding the whole symbol from the live entry cost a documented function its description, its parameter types and its return type after **any** run — and a `SymbolInfo.type()` for anything invocable means its RETURN type, so a live function reporting `function` is not less information than the file had, it is wrong information replacing right information. It took `join('a', 1).` down with it, because a call's type is its callee's |
| **A host binding must reach the ANALYSER, not only the executor** | It is declared nowhere in the file and is not one of the language's own, so without it every binding a mod offers is drawn `variable.unresolved`, offered a rename and an offer to declare it as a local, and hovers as nothing — in the one editor whose own runtime provides it. The `variable.global` capture the token vocabulary reserves for exactly this was dead code for a release. `ScriptBindings` already carries each binding's declared Java TYPE, which is what lets `world.` list the Java engine's members before anything has run |
| **A child-side class may name only JDK types, `com.crystalgui.text.*` and the bridge — a shared utility is MOVED, never imported across** | The band loader is child-first over `com.crystalgui.language.*` and carries our own class files, so a child-side class importing `language.java` does not fail: it quietly defines a SECOND copy on the far side of the bridge, and two objects of "the same" type stop being assignable. `SimilarNames` was imported that way for a release — harmless, being stateless, and exactly the precedent not worth having. The rule is mechanical: **a class that imports `org.mozilla.javascript` is child-side**. It used to be a TABLE in `js/package-info.java`, because the `Js`/`Rhino` prefixes stopped saying which is which the moment the fix catalog turned out to need the AST — and a table is a thing that rots: it had `JsCompletionProvider` on the wrong side (it imports `java.TypeIndex` and `run.ScriptPolicy`, so it is host) and hedged on six classes with "loaded there in practice". It is now the **directory**: `js.host` and `js.rhino`, one rule each. The six hedged classes were settled by asking who calls them — every caller is child-side, so they are too |
| **A runtime's report describes the text that RAN, and must be converted against it** | A run takes time and the file is edited while it runs, so a row and column the engine reports mean something only against the source it compiled. Converting them against the buffer as it is now moves the squiggle one row per line typed above — the "the conversion is only legal against the document the analysis saw" rule, broken by the one lane added after it was written. The compiled source travels with the compilation; a report whose text has since changed is dropped, because the next run will speak for itself |
| **`Integer.MAX_VALUE` is how `Diagnostic.onRow` spells "to the end of this line", so a column is clamped BEFORE it is added to an offset** | `at + column` overflows to a negative number and the whole mark collapses to a point, which reads as a diagnostic that was never widened rather than as arithmetic. `Rope.pointToOffset` has a test named for this; the runtime-problem lane became its second reader and repeated it |
| **Rhino's application class loader is one definition, and so is "does this class exist"** | The executor and the interop resolver each had their own spelling of the host loader, so the editor's "does this class exist" and the runtime's "load this class" were two questions with two answers about one name. `java.util.Map.Entry` is the case that exposed it: the source spelling is what a script writes, what the popup offers and what the Java probe resolves, while the JVM knows only `Map$Entry` — so the name the editor suggested threw when it ran. `JsLoaders` is the one lookup, and it retries in the nested spelling |
| **A membrane over an engine's wrapper must forward everything the engine dispatches on, not only what it renames** | `RhinoRemapping` translates member names, and the two things it nearly took away have nothing to do with names: `Wrapper`, because `NativeJavaMethod.call` unwraps its receiver through it (a `Scriptable`-only membrane is found by the lookup and rejected by the call), and `SymbolScriptable`, because that is how `Symbol.iterator` reaches a Java `Iterable` — so `for (x of javaList)` worked unmapped and stopped working the moment a mapping existed. A class object is also a `Function`, or `new java.util.ArrayList()` is "not a function". **And nothing downstream may then ask a value what CLASS of wrapper it is**: under a mapping nothing is a `NativeJavaClass`, so `Java.type('…')` was reported as an instance of `java.lang.Class` and printed as `class java.util.ArrayList`. Ask `unwrap() instanceof Class` |
| **The engine loader belongs on the thread for calls INTO the engine, and must come off for the script's own execution** | Rhino needs it up to `initStandardObjects`, which is where the regular-expression engine is resolved and cached. After that the script is calling OUT — into a mod's Java, into CrystalGUI — and leaving a child-first loader as the context loader makes every `ServiceLoader` in the application resolve against the engine's classpath, which is the same class of bug pointing the other way |
| **A completion fallback for "I cannot type this receiver" must fire on NO TYPE, never on an empty member list** | A receiver that resolved to `string` or `Array` and came back empty is a gap in `membersOf`, not an untypable receiver — so the popup offered every global the last run had left AS MEMBERS OF A STRING. The gap was real (the standard prototypes were never read; `getAllIds` is needed, since every prototype member is non-enumerable), and the fallback hid it: a list appeared, so nothing looked broken |
| **A line the GRAMMAR cannot parse decolours the whole file — and whether it does depends on a SEMICOLON** | `import a.b.C;` is what this engine supports and what tree-sitter reads as an ES module declaration expecting a string or a `from`. Measured on one fixture: **with** the terminator the error is contained to its own line and everything below is correct; **without** it — ordinary JavaScript, the language inserts semicolons and authors rely on it — the recovery reaches into the body and `var` two rows down is reported as a `variable`, `new` stops being a keyword. So a fixture written with the semicolon passes against no fix at all, which is how the first version of the test went green while the screenshot still showed the bug. `Grammar#sourceFilter` blanks the statement before the parser sees it — length-preserving, and `TreeSitterTokenizer` **throws** on a filter that is not, because every offset it reports is an offset into what it parsed |
| **...and that filter is declared on `Grammar`, not by the language whose dialect it is** — the dependency can only point that way | The instinct is to have `JsLanguage.register()` install it, and `ExecutionNeedsNoGrammarTest` forbids it: `language.js` — with `.java`, `.run`, `.map` and `.engine` — may not name `language.grammar` at all, because a dedicated server runs scripts and must not load five platform natives to do it. Which leaves `.grammar` naming `JsImports`, so that class is the one in `js.rhino` **both loaders define** — safe only because it is stateless and every value it hands over is a `String`; the moment `Scanned` or `Imported` crossed the bridge the two copies would stop being assignable. And the same blanking, not a second copy of the regex: the executor, the analyser and the grammar run the one `JsImports.blank` |
| **A Rhino `Loop` IS a `Scope`, so the structural "what statement am I in" walk stops on the loop’s own HEADER** | `ForLoop`, `WhileLoop` and `ForInLoop` all extend `Scope` — they have to, since `for (let i …)` declares into one — so a condition’s parent answers yes to "is my parent a statement container" and the walk returns the comparison instead of the loop. Two fixes were wrong at once and neither looked it: "Surround with try/catch" on `while (i < xs.length)` offered to wrap **the condition**, producing a `while` header containing a `try` block, and "Introduce variable" hoisted `xs.length` above the loop — evaluated once, so the loop never terminates. A **hang** from an edit the author accepted without reading. `JsQuickFixes.enclosingStatementOf` excludes `Loop` and is now the single walk both fix classes call; the class-based `Block`/`Scope` test was right and the inheritance underneath it was not |
| **A Rhino node’s reported LENGTH is not its text — the `KeywordLiteral` in `this.x` measures FIVE characters** | So reading the source span gives `"this."`, dot and all, and a comparison against `"this"` quietly never matches. The arrow-function conversion was therefore offered on every function that used `this` — precisely the case it exists to refuse, since an arrow inherits `this` lexically and the converted function reads a different object under the same name **and still runs**. Ask `RhinoTokens.keywordOf`, which calls `toSource()` and RENDERS the node rather than measuring it. That class was written for the `Token`-constant divergence and this is a second, unrelated reason to go through it: a span is a claim about the document, and only the node can say what it spells |
| **An operator predicate written as "ends with `=` and does not begin with one" excludes plain `=`** | Which is the case that matters. `xs[i] = 0` was therefore read as a FETCH, so the index loop converted to `for…of` and the body became `x = 0` — assigning to the loop variable and discarding it. The array stops being written, nothing throws, and the diff looks like the refactoring it was advertised as. List the assignment operators; there are sixteen and they are not going to change |
| **Parameter names SURVIVE COMPILATION — the belief that they do not sent a session looking at the wrong artefact** | `src.zip` is a JDK thing and ships with no JRE, so "real parameter names need attached sources" was taken as settled and the production case as lost. Measured: `ArrayList.add` carries `e` in its `LocalVariableTable`, `String.format` carries `format`/`args`, and our own jar carries its names today because Gradle passes `-g`. **Three traps, each silent.** Slot 0 is `this` for an instance method and the FIRST PARAMETER for a static one, and `String.format` is the static case — read it wrong and every name shifts by one into a signature that is plausible and authoritative. `long` and `double` take two slots each. And the table holds every local, not just the parameters: `ArrayList.add(int, Object)` reports `[this, index, element, s, elementData]`. **`-parameters` is not an alternative, it is the other half** — `MethodParameters` is the one attribute needing no `Code` attribute, so it is the only thing that can name an INTERFACE method, which for an SPI-heavy module is most of the surface |
| **`getJavadoc()` answers null until `doc.comment.support` is enabled — and the node covers the comment either way** | Which is the confusing half: `JavaSignatures` had to scan the text to step over a doc comment the accessor denied existed, and recorded that as a workaround. One option turns it on. **It is not a diagnostic switch** — doc-comment *parsing*, not the ~40 javadoc problems, which have their own options and stay off. And **an override must inherit its comment or the feature looks broken on arrival**: an overriding method usually carries `@Override` and nothing else, so without walking to the supertype a large fraction of methods render an empty body, which reads as the feature not working rather than as the method having nothing to say |
| **Retained memory measured as a HEAP DELTA is mostly churn; measured by nulling the reference is zero — measure it by SCALING** | Both are wrong and neither looks it. A delta bracketing a build counts every `ZipFile` structure and short-lived string the collector has not reached: the type index read **~14MB for 11k entries**, about 1.3KB per record of three strings, which would have put it at four times §7.3's budget. Nulling the local and re-measuring reads **~0KB**, because the caller's own variable still holds it. Build several and hold them all — churn does not multiply and retention does, exactly. The real figure was **~331 bytes**, so a full index is ~18MB against a ~20MB budget |
| **`registerCommands` runs from `UIElement`'s INSTANCE INITIALISER, so a per-INSTANCE registration placed there passes `null`** | Its own javadoc says fields are not initialised yet and calls the hazard deliberate, and it is still the shape that gets reached for, because the method reads as "where this widget wires itself up". `Workbench` contributed its `ProjectIndex` there, `ProjectSourcesRegistry.contribute` opens with `if (provider == null) return`, and the whole of M15 S4 was dead on arrival: every cross-file reference in the workspace reported *cannot be resolved to a type* while the registry, the name environment, the project tier and 2,533 tests were all correct. Nothing threw and nothing logged, because **"no provider" and "a provider that knows nothing" are the same answer from outside**. It is also once per CLASS, so even non-null it would register only the first workbench's index. Register per-instance things in the constructor; this hook may name statics only |
| **A bound method reference on a MUTABLE field captures the value, not the field** | `bufferSnapshot::get` binds whatever the field pointed at when the reference was created -- in a constructor, the empty map -- so every later snapshot is invisible to it. It compiles, it reads exactly like an accessor, and the failure is silent: the open-buffer tier never answered, so an unsaved edit lost to the file on disk. The two lines beside it were lambdas and worked. Write `path -> field.get(path)` whenever the receiver can be reassigned |
| **A project package must be reported in the UNNAMED module, never the classpath's** | `ScriptNameEnvironment.getModulesDeclaringPackage` answered for workspace packages by borrowing whatever module `java/lang` belongs to -- correct for the live tier, whose bytes really do come from the classpath, and wrong for a source file compiled here. On a JRT that is `java.base`, so `com.example.util` was attributed to `java.base` while `com.example` -- the compiled unit's OWN package -- was unnamed. **ECJ discards a package chain split across two modules without a word**: it never asks for the type, it just looks for the simple name in the current package. Reported as *The import com.example.util cannot be resolved* while this environment was answering that the package exists. It hides whenever the importer sits in an UNRELATED package, because then every segment is invented here and all of them are consistently wrong -- which is why a cross-package import test passed for a release while `com.example.Main` importing `com.example.util` did not |
| **A PLAYER IS NOT THE ENTITY THEY ARE WEARING — 1.7.10 builds a new `EntityPlayerMP` on every respawn and every dimension change** | `respawnPlayer` constructs a fresh entity, copies the old one into it, and `NetHandlerPlayServer` re-points `playerEntity` at the replacement; the socket, the channel and the handler are untouched, and every map keyed on the entity has just been orphaned. **Inbound dies permanently** — frames name the new body, the lookup misses, and every packet from that client is dropped for the rest of the session. **Outbound keeps working**, because the stale entity retains its handler reference and FML resolves the `PLAYER` target through it at send time, so deltas still arrive and the panel still animates: it reads as an input bug in the widget rather than an identity bug in a map. **And the cleanup misses too** — `PlayerLoggedOutEvent` carries the new body, so an entity-keyed removal removes nothing and the connection, its multiplexer, the workspace binding and the presence entry outlive the player. The identity is the `GameProfile` UUID; the ROUTE is the `NetHandlerPlayServer`, which is what outlives the entity and gets re-pointed, so a send **resolves** its target rather than capturing one. `Mc1710Peer` is both. No test can see any of this — it lives in FML's respawn path |
| **A PER-TICK "COPY THE MODEL INTO THE WIDGETS" METHOD IS A LIST YOU CAN FORGET TO ADD TO, AND FORGETTING LOOKS CORRECT** | The shape every server-side panel was written in — `mirror(model)` called from `tick()`. It works, and its failure is silent in the worst way: a widget nobody remembered to write keeps the value it was BUILT with, which is usually right, so the panel opens correct and then never moves. Nothing throws and nothing logs. Three further costs come with it — a nested model is walked by hand, a COLLECTION cannot be expressed at all (it can re-set what it has and cannot say "these forty items became forty-one"), and a window nobody is watching pays for the whole walk anyway, because `tick()` runs regardless. `ui.projection` replaces it: the pair is stated once, the engine compares and writes, and the set is skipped while no viewer is looking. **The cost was never the comparison** — one `equals` per DISPLAYED field, and a five-hundred-field model behind a twelve-control panel has twelve projections |
| **...and a projection must be SEEDED BEFORE THE DESCRIPTION IS TAKEN, or the window opens visibly wrong and corrects itself** | `open()` encodes the tree as it stands, so a projection that has only ever run from the tick loop leaves the first description carrying whatever the panel's CONSTRUCTOR built. Every projected field then arrives one state delta later. It is exactly why `MachinePanel.serve` used to end with a hand-written `mirror(model)` call, and running the set once between `binder.accept(...)` and `session.open()` is what let that call be DELETED rather than renamed |
| **A HIDDEN VIEWER MAY MISS STATE AND MAY NEVER MISS STRUCTURE** | Per-viewer visibility looks symmetric and is not. A tree delta **renumbers both sides**, so withholding one from a viewer that is not watching leaves it addressing elements by numbers the server has moved on from — and every message after that lands somewhere plausible and incorrect, because an id is an int and they all still resolve to something. Structure therefore goes to every viewer regardless, which costs nothing real: structure changes are rare and state deltas are the per-tick traffic. State is the opposite — keyed by id, so skipping it costs a hidden viewer only freshness |
| **...and a viewer coming back needs the CURRENT state, which a delta cannot give it** | A delta only says what moved, so replaying nothing leaves a returning viewer showing whatever was true when it looked away: correct-looking and stale. It is re-described instead, which is the LATE-VIEWER path and right for the same reason — a live description carries the ids the server is already using, so nothing renumbers. **The description has to be rebuilt first**, and that is the part that is easy to miss: a reshape rebuilt it and a plain STATE change did not, so the returning viewer was handed the tree as it stood at `open()` and resynced to values from however long ago. It reads as a stale delta rather than a stale description |
| **WHAT A LEGAL GESTURE COULD HAVE PRODUCED IS SANITIZED; WHAT IT COULD NOT IS REFUSED** | The whole shape of server-side validation, and each half is wrong applied to the other. A forged out-of-range slider value is CLAMPED and delivered, because the clamped value is one a user could have made — so the handler runs and the model stays sane, where refusing would need a second channel to explain itself and make every handler defensive. A disabled element, an inert one or a kind nobody asked for is REFUSED, because no gesture produces those: the report came from a client that is wrong or a peer that is lying, and the safe reading is the same for both. **The sanitizing belongs to the WIDGET** — a slider's bounds and step, a field's maximum length, a dropdown's option count are known nowhere else, which is precisely why it cannot live in the session. `NaN` is the case worth naming: `Math.max(0, Math.min(1, NaN))` is `NaN` and a model's `==` guard is false against it, so the model is poisoned and every viewer's bars read `NaN` for good |
| **A REFUSAL COUNTER IS PER VIEWER, AND ITS THRESHOLD STOPS THE VIEWER RATHER THAN THE WINDOW** | One refusal is noise — a delta in flight when a control was disabled is ordinary and racy — and a hundred is a client that disagrees about the tree or is not being driven by a user. Counting separates them. Per viewer, or one bad peer closes a window for everyone watching it; and the threshold ends that viewer's participation only, because the window belongs to the others. Minecraft kicks on packet flood and Chromium's `ReportBadMessage` kills the sending renderer; neither takes the document down |
| **A RATE POLICY MAY DROP INTERMEDIATE VALUES AND MAY NEVER DROP THE LAST ONE** | Which is the whole difference between a debounce and data loss: a slider released between ticks would otherwise report a value it was merely passing through. So a held report is never discarded when a newer one lands, only replaced, and it is always eventually sent. **Driven by the CONNECTION's tick**, because a client session riding one does not own a tick of its own — a held value with nothing to flush it never leaves at all, and the failure is a control that works except when you stop moving it |
| **A HOOK NAMED FOR A STATE SAYS NOTHING ABOUT WHEN IT RUNS** — `bound()` became `bindWidgets()` | The salvageable half of D9's merge. Both hooks stay, because they genuinely run at different times, and what was actually wrong is that `bound()` named a condition the panel is IN rather than the job to do in it — so nothing in the name said "attach your widget listeners here, again, every time the tree is replaced", which is the one thing a reader has to know or their listeners end up on widgets that no longer exist. `bindWidgets()` says it, and reads as the counterpart to `client(io)` running once |
| **A REASON THAT MEANS DIFFERENT THINGS ON THE TWO SIDES IS WORSE THAN NO REASON** | `Networked.closed(String)` handed the server a `CloseReason` NAME and the client the wire's human-readable detail — the javadoc said so outright, as though it were a design. So one panel class, asked one question, saw `"NOT_VALID"` on one side and `"no longer valid"` on the other, and any teardown that branched on it worked on exactly one. It is `closed(CloseReason)` on both now, with the enum promoted OUT of `ServerWindow` (a client naming a server class to hear about its own teardown is backwards) and a machine-readable **code** on the wire beside the detail. **Both still travel**, because the two consumers want different halves and neither is wrong: a panel branches on the code, a host shows the detail — "the block was broken" is what a player should read and `SERVER` is not. `parse` answers `UNKNOWN` across a version gap rather than throwing, a window ending being a poor moment to fail |
| **`bound()` AND `client(io)` ANSWER DIFFERENT QUESTIONS AND MUST NOT BE MERGED** — one runs on every bind, the other on the first | Only visible from `ClientWindows.bindPanels`, and D9 asked for the merge anyway. Run the merged hook ONCE and widget listeners are not re-attached after a re-describe, which is the exact bug `bound()` exists to prevent — a delta replaces the tree and every listener is left on widgets that no longer exist. Run it EVERY time and wire methods register twice, which `MessageRouter` refuses outright, correctly, since a duplicate handler is one registration reaching inside another. Making it work would mean weakening the duplicate refusal that catches that very mistake |
| **A SERVER→CLIENT BINDING SHOULD READ LIKE THE CLIENT→SERVER ONE, AND WHEN IT DOES NOT, PEOPLE STOP AT IT** | `io.on(power, Switch.TOGGLE, handler)` and `io.project(power, Switch.CHECKED, model::isRunning)` are one idea pointing two ways, and the first shipped version of the second did not look like it — `project(name, from, to)`, with the widget nowhere in the signature. It took a reader ten minutes to place, which is the whole review finding: an API that resembles an existing one and then differs costs more than an unfamiliar one. **Naming the widget first is not cosmetic** — it is what lets `autoProject` recognise what is already covered by IDENTITY, which deleted two invisible rules with it (name your explicit projections; declare them before the automatic pass), each of whose only symptom when broken is a widget written twice a tick by two projections that may disagree, the later one winning |
| **A LIST PROJECTION MAY SKIP AN UNCHANGED ROW ONLY WHEN THE MODEL HANDED OVER A DIFFERENT INSTANCE** | Given the same instance twice, a value comparison cannot separate "nothing changed" from "mutated in place": a record is immutable and a plain POJO is not, and `equals` on a mutable row object is usually identity — so it answers EQUAL for an item whose fields have just changed. Skipping there freezes the row silently, which is the failure the whole mechanism exists to remove. So a fresh-but-equal item is proof the model re-snapshotted and is skipped; the same instance is re-applied, landing on idempotent widget setters and therefore costing comparisons rather than traffic. The conservative direction is the one that fails LOUDLY (a little wasted work) instead of quietly (a frozen row) |
| **A LISTENER ON A MODEL OUTLIVES THE WINDOW THAT REGISTERED IT** | `bind` connected to a model `Property` and dropped the returned `Connection`. The model is the long-lived thing, so the chain runs model → listener → widget → whole window tree, retained for good after close. The ordinary shape of a listener leak, and the ordinary fix: keep the connection and disconnect it where the window ends. Worth pairing with `Projections.close()` — a projection holds the model, the widget and its last value, and a keyed one holds every realised row, none of which is a leak while the window is discarded whole, but all of which is only true rather than assumed once something says so |
| **A CONVENTION MAY NOT GUESS WHICH SLOT A WIDGET MEANS — the contract has to declare it** | `Slider` carries `MIN`, `MAX`, `STEP` and `VALUE`, all four of them floats, so neither "the first slot" nor type matching can choose — and declaration order deliberately puts the range FIRST, because a value applied before its range is clamped against the old one. A convention that guessed would therefore be wrong on the widget it is most useful for. `WidgetContract.primary()` is the declaration and `null` is a real answer: `Dialog`'s title, `SplitView`'s weights and `Popover`'s mode are not "what the widget is" the way a slider's value is, so those are REPORTED as unwireable rather than assumed |
| **...and the report of what a convention SKIPPED is the feature, not the decoration — but a report that lists everything is a wall nobody reads** | Both halves were learned in one sitting. Auto-wiring that silently misses a field is the frozen-widget failure above wearing a nicer hat, so the misses must be stated (*"live and inert look identical, so a capability that can be silently skipped must say it is on"*). Then the first version stated ALL of them: seventeen lines on `MachinePanel`, fifteen being buttons, which is the sort of output a reader learns to scroll past — taking the two lines that mattered with it. Split by whether there is anything to ACT on: a field the model has no accessor for was never a candidate and is COUNTED, while a field whose accessor exists and still could not be wired is NAMED with its reason. Seventeen lines became one |
| **...and a walk up a panel's superclasses must stop before `UIElement`, which has widget-typed FIELDS OF ITS OWN** | `parent` and `popoverInvoker` are `UIElement`'s own plumbing, so a reflection walk that runs to `Object` reports the element's internals as unwired UI. `UiType.collect` has always stopped at `Networked` levels for this reason and the first `AutoProjection` javadoc claimed running past them was *deliberate* — it was not, and the evidence was in the report the moment it was pointed at a real panel. The boundary is passed IN by the caller, because `ui.projection` cannot name `Networked` without inverting the dependency |
| **A REORDER IS A `move`, AND THE TREE COULD NOT SPELL ONE** — `addChildAt` THROWS on a same-parent move | `hasChild` raises *"Cannot add the same child twice"*, which is correct and stays: it has caught three real double-parenting bugs (`CrystalEditor`, `StatusBarView`, `DockGroup`), each of which would otherwise have become a silent reorder. But it left the wire's `move` op — carried since M2 — with nothing on the engine side able to produce one for a container reordering its own children. So a move is spelled as a move: `UIElement.moveDescribedChildTo`, which reports `moved`. The target index is read AFTER the child is taken out of the list, which is the only reading under which "move it to position 2" means the same thing from either side. Without it a keyed list projection's reorder reaches the far side as destroy-and-rebuild, losing the row instance and everything in it |
| **A GENERIC SEAM NOTHING GENERIC IS WRITTEN AGAINST IS NOT A SEAM, AND EVERY TEST PASSES EITHER WAY** | `ui.dom` was built generic — `TreeSource<N>`, `TreeObserver<N>`, `NodeContract` — for one stated reason: *"the mirror is written once; the engine swap underneath it is a port of the seam's implementation, not of the mirror"*. The mirror was then written as `ServerUiSession implements TreeObserver<UIElement>` holding a concrete `ElementTreeSource`, and **nothing could tell**: there is only one tree to mirror, so a mirror welded to it behaves identically to one that is not. The bill arrives a milestone later as a rewrite of op recording, coalescing, id allocation and the integrity check — the exact logic whose three defects were each silent. The cause was a **missing half of the interface**: it declared identity's READING (`idOf`/`peekId`/`byId`) and not its LIFECYCLE (`allocate`/`release`/`assignAt`/`resetIds`), so the one thing a mirror does had nowhere to stand. The only assertion that can see any of this is one that drives the generic code over a **second** implementation — `MirrorIsEngineAgnosticTest` mirrors a twelve-line `Node` class that has never heard of a widget — and it is worth writing on the day the seam is, not on the day the second engine lands |
| **A DIRTY SET THAT IS CLEARED WITHOUT BEING ENCODED IS INDISTINGUISHABLE FROM ONE THAT WAS NEVER FILLED** | `onIdentityDirty` fired, `ServerUiSession` collected the element into `dirtyIdentity`, and the flush **cleared that set and sent nothing**. So `setEnabled(false)` on a live window was correct on the server, absent on the client, and produced no error at any layer — the same hole swallowed every class change and every inline style write, for as long as identity deltas had existed. It survived because *every* observable was right: the server's tree, the observer, the dirty set, the flush's own return. **Encoding it is only half the fix** — the client had no branch to apply an `a`/`y` entry either, so a test asserting the server sent it passes against a client that drops it. Assert on the far side's ELEMENT (`clientChild(1).isEnabled()`), never on the traffic |
| **AN ID IS ALLOCATED AT FLUSH TIME, NOT AT INSERT TIME — so a guard asking "is this described?" is asking about the FUTURE** | Ids are handed out in the order ops are encoded, which is what lets one `insert` op name a whole subtree as a contiguous `base`+`count` rather than an id per node. The cost is that a subtree added and removed inside one tick has **no id at the moment it is removed**, so `removed()`'s "does this have an id? if not, the far side never heard of it" test answers no and returns — leaving the `insert` standing with no `remove` behind it. The client dutifully builds a subtree the server has already destroyed, and is never told to take it away: a row that outlives whatever briefly created it, with nothing thrown anywhere. **The coalescing scan must come FIRST**, and the id test after it |
| **A COUNT COMPUTED AT `open()` DESCRIBES THE WINDOW THAT OPENED, NOT THE ONE A LATE VIEWER IS JOINING** | `expectedElementCount` is the integrity check a joining client runs against what it rebuilt, and it was fixed at open — so a window that had since gained a row quoted a count one short of the description it was serving, and the newcomer **refused the whole window** rather than one row of it. Recomputed on every reshape. The mirror case is what the check is FOR and is now the only thing it catches: two sides that genuinely build a different number of described elements, from a registry or codec disagreement. It no longer sees internal children at all — those stopped being numbered, so a client whose widget constructor builds one more internal label than the server's is now harmless, which is what it always should have been |
| **A PRISTINE DESCRIPTION AND A LIVE ONE ANSWER DIFFERENT QUESTIONS, AND ONLY ONE OF THEM CAN BE CONTENT-ADDRESSED** | A pristine description carries no ids, so both sides derive them from one document-order walk — which is exactly what makes it hashable and shareable: two windows showing the same thing hash the same, so re-opening costs one small packet however large the tree. Once a window has been reshaped a walk no longer reproduces the numbering the existing viewers hold, so a viewer joining afterwards must be **told** the ids (`UIDescriptionCodec.encodeLive`/`decodeLive`), or every id it derived names a different element and no message lands where it was meant to. A live description hashes to something no pristine one matches, which is correct rather than unfortunate — a reshaped window was never going to share another window's cache entry |
| **A DELTA THAT ARRIVES BEFORE THE TREE EXISTS USED TO BE DROPPED, AND THE LOSS IS PERMANENT** | `ui/openWindow` carries a hash, so unless the description is cached the client must *ask* for it — and nothing tells the server the far side is not ready, so it goes on flushing. Both delta handlers began `if (… \|\| root == null) return;`. Worse than a dropped frame: `Property.set` returns early on an unchanged value, so a widget the server has already written is **never marked dirty again**, and the client shows the description's value forever for exactly the fields that changed early. The first tick is when a window mirrors its model, so this is the common case rather than a corner. Deltas are now queued and replayed **in arrival order** (a state delta computed after a renumber must be applied after the tree delta that caused it) and drained *before* `onWindowOpened`, so a host mounting the tree sees the state already sent rather than one frame of catching up |
| **EVERY STATE SETTER MUST BE IDEMPOTENT, because "mirror the model each tick" is the shape every server-side panel is written in** | `MachinePanel.mirror`'s javadoc states the rule — an unchanged value writes no candidate and marks nothing dirty, so mirroring more often than necessary costs comparisons rather than traffic — and `ProgressBar.setFraction` was the one setter for which it was **false**: it called `notifyStateChanged()` unconditionally, plus `invalidateStyleMatch()` and an IMPORTANT-origin write. So a panel mirroring a bar every tick sent a `ui/stateDelta` **per tick, forever**, carrying a value nobody had moved, and re-ran selector matching on the element every frame. Hidden for as long as every panel gated its mirror behind a dirty flag of its own — which is what makes it a trap rather than a slip, because the flag is the thing the rule says you should not need. The first panel to mirror unconditionally, on exactly those documented grounds, turned a quiet window into constant traffic **and nothing failed**: every one of those deltas carried the correct value. Assert on TRAFFIC (`InMemoryTransport.sent()`), never on state, or the test passes against the bug. `Dropdown` was checked and is fine — `select` guards, and `addOption`/`clearOptions` genuinely mutate. And the guard needs a `applied` flag rather than a bare equality test, because `ProgressBar`'s CONSTRUCTOR calls `setFraction(-1f)` while the field already holds `-1f` |
| **APPLYING A STATE DELTA RUNS THE WIDGET'S ORDINARY SETTER, WHICH FIRES THE WIDGET'S ORDINARY SIGNAL — and that signal is exactly what the reported-event wiring is attached to** | So the server moving a slider made every client that received it tell the server the **user** had moved it: one `ui/event` per viewer, for a gesture nobody made. It survived because it is harmless *in the common case and only there* — the echo carries the value the server just sent, so the handler writes the model back to what it already holds and `Property.set` returns early. It stops being harmless the moment a handler COUNTS anything or records who did it, and with two viewers it is attributed to the **wrong player**. `ClientUiSession.shouldSuppress` is the narrow ancestor of the fix — it stops a delta landing on a focused text field and resetting the caret, the same loop noticed from the one place it was visible — and it stays, because it stops the VALUE arriving rather than the report leaving. **Nothing could see it until something made the server move a REPORTING widget on its own**: every earlier server-side write went to a `ProgressBar` or a `UIText` (which report nothing) or wrote back a value the client had just sent (so the setter no-ops), which is why a suite of 1400 tests and a worked example both missed it. The covering test needs two viewers and a positive control — a guard written as "never report" passes every assertion about the echo and makes every control in the application dead |
| **SUPPRESSING A HIDDEN WINDOW'S TRAFFIC MUST GATE THE WHOLE FLUSH, NEVER ONLY THE SEND** | A tree delta **renumbers both sides**. Gate below `flushStructure` — which is the obvious spelling and reads as equivalent — and this peer renumbers while the other does not, so every state delta afterwards lands on the wrong element, silently, because an id is an int and every one of them still resolves to something. Gating above both means the tree is simply not re-described while nobody is looking and the client's numbering stays the one it was last told. `aWindowReshapedWhileHiddenComesBackWithItsNumberingIntact` is mutation-checked against exactly that move |
| **`session.on` REFUSES a duplicate `(element, kind)`, and used to replace it silently** | A bare `Map.put`: the second registration won and which lambda ran was decided by registration order — the failure `MessageRouter`'s own duplicate check exists to prevent one layer down. It matters most for composition, where it is a boundary rather than a typo: a parent wiring an element its child already wired has reached inside the child, and the two behaviours cannot both run. A parent that wants to KNOW rather than to intercept takes a plain Java callback from the child |
| **...and the register-after-open rule was broader than its own reason** | It refused everything after `open()`, justified by *"the set of reported events is part of the description the client has already been sent"* — true of an element the client HAS been sent, and a tree delta re-describes a subtree in full with its reported events, which `ClientUiSession.wireReportedEvents` already re-wires. The honest test is whether the element has ever been **numbered** (`getNetworkId() >= 0`). Without the relaxation nothing can be added to a live window at all: no fragment, no row, no lazily built page |
| **A RAW NUL BYTE in a `.java` file makes git treat the whole file as binary** | `AttachedSources` used one as a cache-key sentinel (`key.add("\0jdk-sources=" + ...)`), deliberately and for a good reason -- no filesystem permits one in a path. Written as a raw byte rather than an escape it costs the file its diff, its blame and every future review, and it is invisible in every editor so nothing says why. It survived because a binary file simply stops being read. Escapes cost nothing |
| **A `transform` list applies its RIGHTMOST function first, and a single function cannot tell you which way you read it** | `SvgTransform.parse` composed left to right for as long as it existed and every shipped icon carries at most one function, so nothing could disagree. The idiom that can is scaling about a point — `translate(36 32) scale(0.42) translate(-32 -32)`, which is what Illustrator and Inkscape write for every nested group — and read the wrong way it put the logo's gem at `(-3, -5)`: off the canvas, no error, an icon that simply lacked a part. `SvgTransformOrderTest` pins the spec's own equivalence (`translate(10,20) scale(2)` **is** `matrix(2 0 0 2 10 20)`); the tree composition in `SvgResolver` was always right, which is why one level of `<g transform>` worked and a chain did not |
| **Whether an icon needs a TILE or IS one is readable off the file — `SvgDocument.usesCurrentColor()` — and must not be declared beside the name** | A chrome mark is authored as `currentColor` so the cascade can colour it; a brand mark names every colour it uses and must never be tinted. `WindowIcon` puts a palette tile under the first and knocks the glyph out white; under the second that draws a square behind a square. So artwork wears `__branded__` instead of a tile class and paints edge to edge (`overlay-origin: border-box` — the context's padding is the inset a knocked-out glyph needs, not one artwork does). `crystalgui:logo` is the first, and a pack dropping in its own gets the right treatment from the file alone. The design brief for the mark is in the SVG's own header comment: filled polygons and gradients only, no strokes, no `currentColor`, one shard placed three times through `<g transform>` — which is what made the transform-order row above a shipped dependency rather than a latent one |
| **An arc's segment count is per QUARTER TURN, never per command** | `SvgPath.arc` gave every arc the same `steps` whatever its sweep, so at the 8-step tile tier a rounded corner and a whole circle were both eight segments — the corner smooth, the circle an octagon, and a 296° letterform a rough polygon beside perfectly round tile corners. Invisible in the shipped set because Feather rounds corners with quarter arcs and nothing drew a large one small. Normalised on the quarter with `round` rather than `ceil`, so a corner is byte-for-byte what it was and float error cannot add a segment |

---

# Global coding rules

## Port, don't reinvent

**Anything that has been solved thousands of times — text editing, cursor movement, click and drag
selection, undo coalescing, layout — is ported from a battle-tested source and fine-tuned for this
codebase. It is not derived from first principles.**

These behaviours are *conventions, not derivable answers*. Each is one line, each is invisible when
wrong, and each was learned by shipping to millions of users. Four from `text/cursor/` alone:

| Rule | What happens without it |
|---|---|
| Auto-close fires on an **allowlist** (`;:.,=}])> \n\t`), never a denylist | "suppress before a letter" still opens a pair before `$foo` and `#define` |
| A plain arrow collapses a selection to its **edge**, regardless of which way the gesture went | Left-then-right on a backwards selection walks the caret instead of collapsing |
| A partly-commented block **comments out**, it does not half-toggle | Selecting a block with one commented line inverts half of it |
| A backwards word-drag **unions with the anchor word** | Word-granularity drag eats into the word it started on and stops feeling like words |

### Licences are load-bearing here

| Source | Licence | What you may do |
|---|---|---|
| VS Code / Monaco, CodeMirror 6 | **MIT** | **Port the code.** Attribute in the class javadoc, naming the source file. |
| **Chromium** | **BSD-3-Clause** | **Port the code.** Attribute in the class javadoc and in `THIRD-PARTY.md`. `RateEstimator` is one. |
| **Zed**, **wget** | **GPL** | **Read for shape only.** Copying would impose GPL on this repository. `Rope`/`TextSummary` take `SumTree`'s *design*; the progress channel takes wget's *refresh the ETA about once a second" and not a line of its code. |

### Port the module boundaries too

`com.crystalgui.text.cursor` takes its boundaries from VS Code, though **not file-for-file** — the
mapping is worth stating because two of the five come from elsewhere in that tree:

| Ours | VS Code |
|---|---|
| `CursorColumns` | `common/core/cursorColumns.ts` |
| `MoveOperations` | `common/cursor/cursorMoveOperations.ts` |
| `TypeOperations` | `common/cursor/cursorTypeOperations.ts` |
| `LineOperations` | `contrib/linesOperations/browser/linesOperations.ts` — **not** `common/cursor/` |
| `MouseSelection` | `browser/controller/mouseHandler.ts` — **not** `common/cursor/` |
| `ColumnSelection` | `common/cursor/cursorColumnSelection.ts` |

Monaco's `common/cursor/` holds twelve files; the six we have no counterpart for are its orchestration
layer (`cursor.ts`, `cursorCollection.ts`, `oneCursor.ts`, `cursorContext.ts`, `cursorMoveCommands.ts`)
plus **one** remaining feature gap: `cursorAtomicMoveOperations.ts` — `editor.useTabStops` for *arrows*,
where a left or right arrow steps a whole indent unit through leading whitespace. Backspace already does
(`TypeOperations.backspaceFrom` counts visual columns), which is the half that was reported; the arrows
still move by one character.

> **Every column question in this package is a VISUAL one**, and that is the single most portable thing
> about it. A box selection computed from character offsets is not a box — two rows whose indentation
> differs in tabs have the same character column at different places, so the "rectangle" comes out as a
> ragged edge following the text. The same rule is why Backspace takes one tab rather than an
> indent's worth of characters, and why a paste is re-indented by columns rather than by string length.

> This is not tidiness. The same logic first went in as private methods on `TextEditor`, which reached
> **2556 lines, larger than the entire `com.crystalgui.text` package combined**, and could only be
> reached through a `UIWindow` with fonts, a style engine and an input handler. Extracting it exposed a
> real bug within minutes — deleting the *last* line left a blank line, because the last row has no
> trailing newline to take and must swallow the one before it instead. The widget test never caught it:
> it only ever deleted a middle line. **Porting the algorithms without the boundaries keeps the
> algorithms and throws away the testability that keeps them correct.**

## CrystalGraphics ownership boundary

**CrystalGraphics owns the rendering backend. CrystalGUI consumes it.**

- CrystalGUI may define renderer-facing abstractions and UI draw orchestration.
- Fonts, shaders, framebuffers, VAO/VBO, draw submission, GPU resource ownership, and modern GL
  pipeline capability belong in **CrystalGraphics**.
- Never write raw GL, raw `float[]` vertex packing, or a hand-rolled buffer in `core/`.

Because CrystalGraphics lives in this repo and is directly writable, if CrystalGUI needs a new backend
capability we **add it to CrystalGraphics** and integrate against the new API — we do not reimplement
the backend here.

> The full infrastructure ownership map (`CgStreamBuffer`, `CgStagingBuffer`, `CgVertexWriter`,
> `CgInstanceWriter`, `CgBufferWriter`, `CgShaderBuffer`, `CgMesh`, `CgVertexArray`,
> `CgAbstractShaderProgram`, and the decision tree for picking between them) lives in
> **`CrystalGraphics/AGENTS.md`**, which `CLAUDE.md` already loads every session. Read it there;
> it is not duplicated here.

## Platform-agnostic core

`core/` must stay fully cross-platform — the import guard enforces no `net.minecraft.*`,
`cpw.mods.fml.*`, `net.minecraftforge.*`, `org.lwjgl.*`.

**The platform seam is CrystalGraphics'.** CrystalGUI has no registry of its own — it reads everything
through `CgPlatform`, and a loader registers exactly one `CgPlatformService` bundle:

| Need | Reached via | Lives in |
|---|---|---|
| Key/mouse codes, modifier state, **and the clipboard** | `CgPlatform.input()` | `platform/service/CgInputService` |
| UI sounds | `CgPlatform.sound()` | `platform/service/CgSoundService` |
| Presenting a cursor | `CgPlatform.cursor()` | `platform/service/CgCursorService` |
| Raw event sink (`UIInputHandler` implements it) | — | `platform/input/CgSystemInput` |
| Code constants, cursor enum, cursor artwork | — | `platform/input/CgKeyCodes`, `CgMouseCodes`, `CgModifiers`, `CgCursor`, `CgCursorBitmaps` |

> **The clipboard is on `CgInputService`, not a service of its own.** It is not conceptually input, but it
> is reached the same way and needed by exactly the code that handles keys — two methods do not earn a
> registration slot. Both default to a no-op pair.

**No method in this SPI has a default, and neither sound nor cursor ships a `NOOP` constant.** A default is
an answer chosen for someone who never saw the question: a new platform compiles cleanly while silently
inheriting "no sound, no cursor, no clipboard", and inheriting a no-op is indistinguishable from deciding
on one. Abstract methods make the compiler the reminder — and a platform with nothing to offer still says
so, with an empty body in its own source.

> **Why this stopped being CrystalGUI's own registry.** `CrystalGuiCore` used to hold four static fields
> with setters. CrystalGraphics is the parent project and is always present, so two registries meant a
> loader had to find both — and could wire up one, leaving a UI with a working GL backend and no keyboard.
> One bundle makes a platform either registered or not. `CrystalGuiCore` now holds only `LOGGER`.

## Lombok

Prioritize Lombok to eliminate handwritten accessor boilerplate. It generates Java 8-compatible
bytecode and is `compileOnly` — no runtime dependency.

| Annotation | Use when |
|---|---|
| `@Data` | Simple POJOs, all fields in equals/hashCode/toString |
| `@Getter` / `@Setter` | Selective access — apply at field level when only some fields need accessors |
| `@RequiredArgsConstructor` | Immutable classes — pairs with `@Getter` only |
| `@Builder` | 4+ constructor parameters, or many optional ones |
| `@Value` | Fully immutable data carriers |
| `@ToString` / `@EqualsAndHashCode` | When you need one without full `@Data` |
| `@Accessors(chain = true)` | Fluent setters — used widely here |

Do **not** use `@Data` on classes with inheritance — use explicit annotations and always
`@EqualsAndHashCode(callSuper = true)` on subclasses.

---

# Package map

```
com.crystalgui.core            CrystalGuiCore — the global LOGGER, and nothing else. The platform
                               registry it used to hold now lives in CrystalGraphics; see below.
  .data                        CacheCell / IntCacheCell / LongCacheCell (dirty-flag memoization),
                               ReadOnlyVec2f (immutable view over a mutable JOML Vector2f), Transform2D
  .dispose                     Disposable, Disposable.Gl, Disposer — the ownership tree. NOT a
                               replacement for CgGraphicsLifecycle's registry sweep; it exists to
                               release on CLOSE rather than on exit, and to reach createOwned GL
                               objects no registry can see. docs/CGUI_WORKBENCH_SERVICES.md
  .property                    Property<T> (binding, equality-suppressing set), ObservableList<T>
  .signal                      Signal.Action/Value/Pair, SignalBase, Connection, ConnectionGroup
  .command                     Command (a named invocable action), CommandContext, CommandRegistry —
                               what a key binding, a menu item and the palette all point at. Plus the
                               MENU MODEL: MenuId (a named place a menu is drawn, interned, with nested
                               submenus), MenuSection (a group + its rows — what a separator is drawn
                               from), MenuEntry (Item/Submenu, sealed; an Item carries enabled/checkable/
                               checked so the RENDERER decides), MenuContributor (rows computed at open
                               time — the Window menu's editor list). CommandRegistry.sections() is the
                               one query every menu renderer reads; menu() is its deprecated flat view
  .undo                        Edit (one undoable change), CompositeEdit, UndoStack — one history per
                               DOCUMENT, never per window

com.crystalgraphics.platform   NOT CrystalGUI's code — CrystalGraphics' platform SPI, which CrystalGUI
                               consumes. Listed here because the engine's input, sound, clipboard and
                               cursor seams all live in it.
  (root)                       CgPlatform (the registry), CgPlatformService (the bundle a loader registers)
  .input                       CgSystemInput (raw Mouse/Keyboard event sink + event types),
                               CgKeyCodes (LWJGL2-shaped, no LWJGL import), CgMouseCodes,
                               CgModifiers (bitmask), CgCursor (the cursor keyword set),
                               CgCursorBitmaps (procedural 32x32 cursor art)
  .service                     CgInputService (codes, modifier/key/button state, AND the clipboard),
                               CgSoundService, CgCursorService — plus CrystalGraphics' own six

com.crystalgui.lifecycle       CgUiLifecycle — the ONE CgLifecycleListener CrystalGUI registers with
                               CrystalGraphics; drives paint-context teardown + cache invalidation

com.crystalgui.render          CgUiPaintContext (singleton), CgUiRenderer, ScissorStack,
                               CgUiBackdrop — the backdrop primitive under glass(): capture the region
                               behind an element, blur it (separable Gaussian at 1/4 res), hand back the
                               sharp and blurred textures with UVs. Sits BESIDE the paint context and
                               reaches it through package-private members, as TextEditor's view parts do
  .text                        FontFamilyCache — (font stack, px) -> CgFontFamily
  .texture                     CgUiDrawable (SPI), CgUiQuad, CgUiSprite (9-slice), CgUiRoundedRect (SDF),
                               CgUiCrossFade, CgUiLayerBox, CgUiRepeat, ArgbMath, CgUiSvg,
                               CgUiGlass (liquid glass — blur, luminosity blend, refraction, specular, noise, over a live
                               backdrop), CornerRadiusAware (the seam that stops a self-clipping drawable
                               being wrapped in a CgUiRoundedRect it cannot survive),
                               CgUiTransformDrawable (stub)
    .svg                       A full SVG renderer, parse to cached draw ops: SvgScanner (nested tags),
                               SvgPath (the d grammar), SvgTransform, SvgColor, SvgStyle (inheritance),
                               SvgGradient, SvgTriangulator (scanline fills, holes cut), SvgDocument
    .asset                     CgUiSpriteRegistry — lazy "ns:name" -> sprite from ui/sprites/*.json;
                               FileIconTheme — VS Code's file-icon-theme model, ui/icons/*.json
    .geometry                  Position, Size

com.crystalgui.style           ElementStyle, StyleGroup, GeneralGroup, LayoutGroup, StyleOrigin,
                               TaffyBridge, PseudoClasses, StyleEngine, CssParsingUtil, CssAngle
  .sheet                       StyleSheet (+DEFAULT), StyleRule, DeclarationParser (var(--x)),
                               StyleSheetRegistry
  .selector                    Selector, CompoundSelector, SelectorType
  .transition                  TransitionEngine, TransitionSpec, ActiveTransition, TransitionValue
  .easing                      Easing, Linear, CubicBezier, LinearPiecewise, ConstantEasing,
                               ProgressFunctions
  .property                    StyleProperty<T>, StylePropertyRegistry, StyleSlot, StyleValue,
                               IValueInterpolator
    .general.{bools,enums,floats,ints,strings}   scalar StyleValue/StyleProperty flavors
    .layout                    LayoutProperties, BoxEdgeShorthands
    .layout.{dimension,grid,length}              Taffy-shaped value types (LPA*, LPSize, Grid*)
    .visual                    Overflow, OverflowClip, ScrollBehavior, BoxOrigin, DrawableAlign,
                               DrawableFit, OutlineShorthand, OutlineOffsetShorthand (per-edge,
                               unlike CSS)
    .visual.border             BorderRadiusProperties, BorderRadiusShorthand, LengthPercent(+Property/Value)
    .visual.color              ColorProperty, ColorValue
    .visual.text               FontFamilyValue
    .visual.texture            TextureProperty, TextureValue
    .visual.transform          TransformProperty, TransformValue, TransformOriginShorthand

com.crystalgui.ui.contract     WHAT A KIND OF WIDGET IS — one declaration, four readers.
                               WidgetContract (name + ordered State slots + Event slots),
                               State<W,V> (a wire key, a getter/setter pair, an omitted-when value and
                               an optional sanitizer — DECLARATION ORDER IS APPLY ORDER, which several
                               widgets depend on), Event<W,P> (a kind, a payload codec, and HOW A
                               CLIENT LISTENS — which is what deleted the instanceof switch),
                               StateType/StateTypes, RatePolicy (immediate/typing/dragging; a widget
                               knows its own tempo and a handler cannot), WidgetContracts (the
                               registry, and the local-only list with reasons). NOTE there is no kind
                               vocabulary class: a kind is a string an Event declares, unique only
                               within its own widget's contract, so a third party mints one without
                               editing anything of ours. plan_ui_rewrite.md M1

com.crystalgui.ui.dom          THE SEAM — the tree as everything above the engine sees it.
                               TreeSource<N> (stable node identity, light-tree iteration, contractOf,
                               one observer), TreeObserver<N> (an EDIT SCRIPT: inserted-with-index,
                               removed, MOVED, attributeChanged, inlineStyleChanged, stateChanged),
                               NodeContract (what a KIND is — name, reported events, whether children
                               may be described), ElementTreeSource (the implementation over today's
                               UIElement, and the file M5 replaces). Written so the mirror is authored
                               ONCE: `ui.dom`'s own node tree implements the same interface natively
                               and nothing above it changes. plan_ui_rewrite.md §0
  .shadow                      SPIKE S2 — ShadowRoot (a real style scope: outer rules cannot reach in,
                               parts are exposed BY NAME through ::part, focus retargets to the host),
                               ShadowButton (the subject: Button rebuilt as host + shadow root, beside
                               the stock one in `cgui-shadow-parts`). What it measures is the cost of
                               replacing encapsulation-by-boolean before 54 composites are ported

com.crystalgui.ui              UIElement, UIWindow, Ui, UITransform, EventListenerGroup,
                               ElementRegistry, UIFrameTicker (SPI), UITreeObserver, TopLayer, UIResizer,
                               AnchoredPlacement (CSS Anchor Positioning subset)
  .projection                  THE MODEL REACHING THE WIDGETS -- the direction none of the three
                               audits covered. Projections (declare a read/write pair once; the engine
                               compares and writes only on change, runs the set before the flush, and
                               skips it entirely while no viewer is watching) + `each` for KEYED lists,
                               where an untouched row keeps its element so an insert is an insert;
                               AutoProjection (panel field name -> model accessor, over the same
                               reflection UiType does) and its Report, which names what it could NOT
                               wire because a convention that skips silently leaves a widget at a value
                               that usually looks right. It is UI-layer, not net-layer: a client-only
                               panel can project a model too. Reached through ServerScope.project /
                               projectEach / projectWhen / autoProject / bind. plan_ui_rewrite.md M3.P
  .tree                        UITreeTraversal — stateless ancestor/tab-order queries
  .event                       UIEvent, PropagationPhase, CloseEvent, DOMEvent, DragEvent, FocusEvent,
                               KeyboardEvent, MouseEvent
  .input                       UIInputHandler, UIDragController, FocusPolicy, ButtonState
  .text                        TextRange, HighlightRegistry — CSS Custom Highlight API (ranges in Java,
                               styling in CSS via ::highlight(name)); see StyleEngine.highlightStyle
  .elements                    Button, Checkbox, CheckboxGroup, Dialog, DialogManager, DragGhost,
                               Dropdown, Menu, MenuItem, Popover, Scroller, ScrollerView, Slider,
                               SplitView, Switch, Tab, TabView, TextField, Tooltip, UIText
    .chrome                    The shell's own widgets, none of them general-purpose: MenuBarView (the
                               main menu bar), MenuBuilder (sections -> rows; the ONE place a Menu is
                               populated from commands, and ContextMenu is a caller of it), ContextMenu,
                               MainMenuCommands, CommandPalette + QuickPick*, StatusBarView,
                               NotificationsView/Balloons/Card, ProblemsPanel + ProblemNode +
                               ProblemsTreeSource, Breadcrumbs, NavigatorView, Preferences, PageStack,
                               InputDialog, ChromeCommands
    .editor                    TextEditor (the widget), EditorCommands (its named actions), plus VS
                               Code's VIEW-PART decomposition: EditorViewPart (the base + Monaco's
                               shouldRender protocol), DecorationPool (the pool/hide idiom), and one
                               part each — LineNumbersPart, ViewCursorsPart, SelectionsPart,
                               CurrentLinePart, IndentGuidesPart, WhitespacePart, RulersPart,
                               GutterEdgePart, FoldingDecorationsPart, ZoomIndicatorPart
    .workbench                 TOOL WINDOWS and the shell's file panel. ToolWindowManager owns which exist,
                               where each belongs and whether it is on screen; ToolWindowState is the
                               per-tool-window record beside the layout (IntelliJ's WindowInfoImpl), and
                               ToolWindowType is how one is PRESENTED — DOCKED in its region, FLOATING in a
                               frame owned by the workbench's window, or WINDOWED in a top-level one.
                               ToolWindowFrame is that frame: an ordinary WindowFrame hosting the same
                               ViewContainer the region shows, with a Dock button beside the window
                               controls and the container's own header adopted into its caption through
                               WindowChrome, so a float has ONE header. It replaced FloatingDock, which
                               extended Dialog, promoted itself to the global top layer, and had no callers.
                               The file panel is split the way VS Code's explorer is: ProjectFileTree
                               is the VIEW (explorerView.ts), FilesRenderer builds and fills a row,
                               ExplorerDragAndDrop is FileDragAndDrop, ExplorerFind is ExplorerFindProvider
                               plus the bar it lacks, ExplorerEditing is setEditable + renderInputBox, and
                               WorkspaceTreeSource is the MODEL (explorerModel.ts) — listings, sorting,
                               compact folders, what matches. The parts sit BESIDE the view and reach it
                               through package-private accessors, as TextEditor's ten view parts do
    .desktop                   CrystalOS — Desktop (the compositor host), WindowFrame (one window),
                               WindowRegistry (every LIVE window, visible or hidden, in two orders: open
                               order for the taskbar, MRU for the switcher — and neither is derivable
                               from the other, since a hidden window has left the stacking order while
                               keeping its place in the sequence), Taskbar (that registry RENDERED, never
                               a second list — laid out along the bottom, entries centred, so the work
                               area is simply what is left above it), WindowState and WindowPolicy.
                               HIDE IS DETACH, which is what makes the freeze real rather than promised:
                               a detached subtree matches no selector, lays out and paints nothing, and
                               has had every input reference dropped by onRemoved. Close is a REQUEST
                               routed through the policy; destroy runs Disposer; retention is bounded by
                               an LRU cap that passes over any window whose content refuses to be
                               discarded. WindowChrome is the client-side-decoration seam: content with
                               a top bar of its own (CrystalEditor's menu bar) offers ONE element, which
                               setContent MOVES into the caption and puts back on release — so a window
                               has one header rather than two stacked on each other.
                               NOBODY CONSTRUCTS ANY OF IT: every UIWindow owns a desktop and hands it out
                               through desktop(), the way it already owns its overlay layer, and a UI
                               opens a window with UIWindow.openWindow(frame). The desktop is an
                               internal child of the root, above the root's own content (the band model:
                               desktop content < windows < pinned < top layer) and ZERO-SIZED until a
                               window is open, so an unused compositor cannot swallow clicks meant for
                               the application under it.
                               The SWITCHER is WindowSwitcher over DesktopCommands -- Mod+Tab, MRU
                               order (the taskbar reads open order; neither is derivable from the other),
                               a grid of live WindowThumbnails so a minimised window shows its snapshot.
                               MOTION is WindowAnimator over two drivers behind WindowMotion:
                               WindowAnimation (transform+opacity, for open/close/minimise -- what a
                               compositor does) and WindowGeometryAnimation (layout, for maximise and
                               restore-down, because a size change REFLOWS and a transform would draw the
                               destination's layout at the source's geometry). PREVIEWS are TaskbarPreviews
                               (hover timing, one panel that moves between entries) over WindowPreview
                               (the panel) and WindowThumbnail, which mirrors a live window's subtree
                               through CgUiPaintContext.mirrored -- or draws WindowSnapshot, the retained
                               FBO photographed on the way into a minimise, for one that is no longer
                               there to draw. plan_windowing.md
    .workbench.decoration      FileDecoration, FileDecorationProvider, FileDecorations — VS Code's
                               IDecorationsProvider. Independent contributors (dirty, read-only, errors,
                               VCS) merged per field, with bubbling up to ancestor folders
    .canvas                    CanvasView (pan/zoom viewport), WorldRect — the node graph's substrate
    .graph                     GraphView, GraphNode, NodePort, NodeWireLayer, GraphConnection,
                               GraphSelection, GraphCommands, PortType (SPI) + BasicPortType +
                               PortTypeRegistry, PortDirection

com.crystalgui.text            Rope, TextBuffer, TextSummary, Change/ChangeSet, Selection,
                               SelectionModel, TextPoint, TextRange, WordClassifier, WordOperations,
                               LineEnding — the document model, all headless. Plus the two utilities
                               BOTH engines need and neither may own: SimilarNames (how close is close
                               enough for a "did you mean") and DerivedNames (a name for something the
                               author has not named). Each was in an engine until the second engine
                               wanted it — a child-side class may not be imported across, so a shared
                               utility is MOVED here rather than referenced in place
  .cursor                      CursorColumns, MoveOperations, TypeOperations, LineOperations,
                               MouseSelection, ColumnSelection — VS Code's boundaries, but NOT
                               file-for-file: MouseSelection and LineOperations come from
                               browser/controller/ and contrib/linesOperations/. See "Port the module
                               boundaries too" for the full mapping and the ONE unimplemented gap left
                               (atomic tab moves for the ARROW keys; Backspace already steps by column)
  .syntax                      Language, LanguageRegistry, SyntaxToken, SyntaxTokenizer (SPI),
                               KeywordTokenizer — the ENGINELESS tier, and what a dedicated server has
  .lang                        The semantic layer's contracts, INTERFACES ONLY: LanguageServices (the
                               per-DOCUMENT facade), SemanticTokenProvider, Resolver, CompletionProvider
                               + CompletionItem/CompletionList, SymbolInfo/SymbolKind/SymbolModifier,
                               TypeRef, DeclarationSite, Versioned, and TypeSearch + TypeSearchRegistry
                               ("which types are on the classpath" — what Go to File asks, inverted for
                               the same reason as the rest: the INDEX lives in language/ and core/ may
                               never name it). Every engine lives in language/; this package is the whole
                               footprint in core/, and its absence at runtime is the only feature flag.
                               docs/CGUI_WORKBENCH_SERVICES.md
  .diagnostic                  Diagnostic, DiagnosticSet, DiagnosticSeverity, DiagnosticTag, Markers,
                               RelatedInformation — LSP-shaped, per-owner. NOT duplicated in .lang
  .wrap                        LineProjection, ProjectedLines, LineBreaksComputer (SPI),
                               MonospaceLineBreaks, ShapedLineBreaks, BreakOpportunities, WrapIndent —
                               soft wrap, and the model/view coordinate seam the whole editor rests on
  .view                        IndentLevels, WhitespaceMarkers, RenderWhitespace
  .fold                        FoldingRegions (+Region), FoldingModel, FoldingRangeProvider (SPI),
                               IndentRangeProvider — folding. INDENT-based by default, which is Monaco's
                               default too and deliberately not brackets; see the class javadoc for why

com.crystalgui.serialization   Codec<A>, DynamicOps<T>, Codecs, CodecException, JsonOps, PlainOps,
                               StateMap, UIDescriptionCodec, ContentHash
  .style                       StyleValueCodecs, InlineStyleCodec

com.crystalgui.net             UITransport, InMemoryTransport,
                               ServerUiSession, ClientUiSession, ClientUiSessions, UiWindowMux,
                               SheetRef  -- ids live in ui.dom.ElementTreeSource, not here
  .mirror                      THE MIRROR, and it names no widget, no session and no transport:
                               ServerTreeMirror<N,T> (observes a TreeSource, records insert/remove/
                               move with coalescing, allocates ids, PRODUCES payloads rather than
                               sending them -- which is what lets one window fan out to viewers with
                               different visibility without this class knowing viewers exist),
                               ClientTreeMirror<N,T> (applies them), NodeMirror<N,T> (the per-tree
                               seam: how a node is described and reconstructed -- BOTH halves on one
                               interface, because an encode with no matching apply is exactly the
                               defect that made identity changes silently never travel, and on one
                               interface that omission is a compile error), ElementNodeMirror over
                               today's UIElement tree, TreeOps (the wire vocabulary). A second engine
                               supplies a TreeSource and a NodeMirror and nothing else
  .window                      A WINDOW'S LIFETIME — the layer above the sessions, and the one a mod
                               uses. Networked<M> (ONE class per UI: a UIElement whose widgets are
                               FIELDS — the field name becomes the id — with layout/serve/tick/
                               stillValid/title/key on the server, bound/client on the client, closed
                               on both; the panel IS the tree's root on both sides, so the mounted
                               root is the panel and `machinepanel { }` styles it by tag), UiType (the
                               identity both sides reference AND the engine's customElements.define —
                               registers the panel's tag so a description decodes into the class;
                               build() on the server, bind() on the client, nested field types
                               registered recursively), ServerScope / ClientScope (what serve() and
                               client() are handed — a VIEW of the window's one session, prefixed by
                               the panel's element-id path, so a nested panel's "save" is
                               "engines/save" on both sides with nobody writing the string;
                               composition is ServerScope.attach(child, slice) — props down, the id
                               is the namespace, and the child panel is built by the PARENT's layout
                               with the slice only it knows), ServerWindow<P> (the final HANDLE
                               open() returns — session, key dedup, close matrix; NOT an authoring
                               surface any more), ServerWindows.open(TYPE, model) — the WHOLE
                               wiring: the open names the panel class on the wire
                               (UiMethods.UI_CLASS) and the client initialises it, GUARDED (loaded
                               without running anything, must be Networked, only then initialised),
                               so there is no client registration at all — WindowMount +
                               ClientWindowContext (the platform seam), SheetSupply (local resolver →
                               cache → ui/sheet). plan_ui_host.md

                               NOTE the three senses of "window", which is why these live in their own
                               package: UIWindow is the ENGINE for one surface (and is the one genuine
                               misnomer — it plays the DOM's Document role and would be UIDocument if
                               it were named today), WindowFrame is the CHROME on a desktop, and
                               ServerWindow is the NETWORKED UNIT. The protocol has said "window" in
                               that third sense since windowId existed.
```

**Naming corrections vs. older notes:** `render/` is top-level (`com.crystalgui.render`), *not* nested
under `core/`. There is **no `core/input/` or `core/sound/` package any more** — the raw platform I/O
layer moved wholesale to `com.crystalgraphics.platform`, and `com.crystalgui.core` is now the logger plus
three small utility packages. Dispatch and focus were always in `ui/input/` and stayed there. The
three-phase event types are in `ui/event/` — there is no `core/event/` package.
